.

For brevity, we only show actions IDs and omit tables IDs.

Figure

2
shows an example of P4Info metadata file for the P4 program in

Section

2.2
. From this metadata we can see that a P4 device running
that program has two tables:
IPv4_table
and
IPv6_table
. Each
table has one key that is an address of the corresponding IP protocol version.
The entries of
IPv4_table
and
IPv6_table
can invoke actions

IPv4_forward
and
IPv6_forward
(respectively) and must provide a
MAC address and port as action’s arguments. All table entries can invoke

Drop_action
, which has no parameters.

P4Runtime applications can change the configuration of a P4-enabled device by
adding, updating, and deleting table entries. P4Runtime applications can also
read the table contents, possibly using
wildcards
to filter the results.
As shown by the Python program in Figure
1
, it is easy
to make mistakes if the language does not perform static checks on table
updates. Specifically, the P4Info metadata in
Figure

2
says that any
new entry added to
IPv4_table
cannot use the
IPv6_forward
action
— which is the mistake highlighted in
Figure

1
.

2.4.
An Overview of Our Approach

To address the issues described above, we propose
P4R-Type
: a verified P4Runtime
API for Scala 3, with a tool to translate P4Info metadata into Scala types. Our
approach is depicted below.

As usual, a P4 data plane program is compiled and deployed on one or more
P4-enabled network devices; the program’s P4Info metadata is made available for
P4Runtime applications. This is where
P4R-Type
comes into play: a programmer
can write a P4Runtime control application by importing (1) the
P4R-Type
library,
and (2) a set of type definitions automatically generated from P4Info metadata.
If the
P4R-Type
-based application type-checks, then it will be able to connect to
a P4 device (acting as P4Runtime server)
and perform P4Runtime operations that never violate the

device configuration — provided that the
configuration matches the P4Info metadata.
The design and implementation of
P4R-Type
is based on a formal model allowing us
to reason about the behaviour of P4Runtime client applications and P4 devices
acting as P4Runtime servers.

Our formal model is outlined above: a P4Runtime server
S
holds tables
and actions that are well-formed w.r.t. a configuration
C
(which represents
P4Info metadata).
We define an encoding from a P4Info configuration
C

into a set of types for
F_{\text{P4R}}
: a formal
calculus describing P4Runtime client applications.
We design the typing discipline of
F_{\text{P4R}}
to include match types and singleton types, which are also present in
the Scala 3 typing system: this design allows us to implement our results as a
Scala 3 API (i.e.,
P4R-Type
) reflecting the typing constraints of
F_{\text{P4R}}
.
Then, we prove our
Theorems

6.1
and
6.4
: if a
F_{\text{P4R}}
program
t
type-checks with types
encoded from a P4Info configuration
C
, then
t
will interact correctly with
any P4Runtime server
S
that is well-formed w.r.t.
C
.

3.
A Model of P4Runtime Clients, Servers, and Networks

We now illustrate how we model P4Runtime networks consisting of P4-enabled
devices (acting as servers), and control applications (the clients) that connect
and modify the devices’ P4 table entries.
In
Section

3.1
we introduce the syntax of
F_{\text{P4R}}
,
a formal language for modelling P4Runtime client programs, with the capability of
connecting to P4Runtime servers and performing P4Runtime operations.
In
Section

3.2
we model P4Runtime servers by focusing on
their internal configuration, i.e. their P4 tables, packet matching methods, and
actions.
In
Section

3.3
we formalise a P4Runtime network as a parallel
composition of P4Runtime clients and servers.

We introduce the semantics of
F_{\text{P4R}}
programs, servers, and networks
later on (in
Section

5
) after introducing the typing system (in

Section

4
).

3.1.
The
F_{\text{P4R}}
Language for P4Runtime Clients

In
Definition

3.1
below we introduce the syntax of the

F_{\text{P4R}}
language and its types.
F_{\text{P4R}}
is designed as an
extension of
F_{<:}
(System F with subtyping
(
Cardelli et al. 1994
)
)
augmented with:

•

P4Runtime-specific operations
: primitives and types for server
addresses and channels;

•

singleton types
, i.e. types inhabited by exactly one value; and

•

match types
, introducing the capability of performing
type-level computations and refining the result type of a pattern matching.
Our match types are based on the work of
Blanvillain et al. 2022
(which in
turn formalises the corresponding feature of the Scala 3 programming
language) — but our adaptation includes significant differences: we discuss
them later on, in
Remark

4.6
.

Definition 3.1 (Syntax of
F_{\text{P4R}}
).

The syntax of
F_{\text{P4R}}
terms
t
and types
T
is shown in

Figure

3
— where
I
(used to index records
and pattern matching terms, and record and match types) represents a finite,
non-empty set containing sequential natural numbers
1,2,\ldots

Moreover,

Figure

4
introduces some frequently-used
syntactic abbreviations.

\small\begin{array}[]{r@{\;\;}rcl@{\quad}l}\text{Term}&t&\Coloneqq&v\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Value)}\\
&&|&x\;\;|\;\;y\;\;|\;\;z\;\;|\;\;\ldots\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Variable)}\\
&&|&{t}\mathbin{\texttt{{:}\!{:}}}{t}\;\;|\;\;\texttt{head }t\;\;|\;\;\texttt{tail }t\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(List constructor, head, tail)}\\
&&|&\text{$\{f_{i}=t_{i}\}_{i\in I}$}\;\;|\;\;t.f\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Record constructor, field selection)}\\
&&|&t\ t\;\;|\;\;t\ T\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Term application, type application)}\\
&&|&\texttt{let }x\texttt{ = }t\texttt{ in }t\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Binder)}\\
&&|&\text{$t\texttt{ match }\{x_{i}:T_{i}\Rightarrow t_{i}\}_{i\in I}$}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Pattern matching)}\\
&&|&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\mathit{op}}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(P4Runtime operation)}}$}}\\[2.13394pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{P4Runtime op.}}$}}&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\mathit{op}}$}}&\Coloneqq&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\texttt{Connect}(w)}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(Connect to server)}}$}}\\
&&|&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\texttt{Read}(w,w)}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(Read entries)}}$}}\\
&&|&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\texttt{Insert}(w,w)}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(Insert an entry)}}$}}\\
&&|&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\texttt{Modify}(w,w)}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(Modify entries)}}$}}\\
&&|&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\texttt{Delete}(w,w)}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(Delete entries)}}$}}\\[2.13394pt]
\text{P4Runtime arg.}&w&\Coloneqq&v\;\;|\;\;x\;\;|\;\;y\;\;|\;\;z\;\;|\;\;\ldots\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Value or variable)}\\[2.13394pt]
\text{Value}&v&\Coloneqq&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${v_{G}}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(Ground value)}}$}}\\
&&|&{v}\mathbin{\texttt{{:}\!{:}}}{v}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(List value)}\\
&&|&\text{$\{f_{i}=v_{i}\}_{i\in I}$}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Record value)}\\
&&|&\lambda x:T.\ t\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Lambda abstraction)}\\
&&|&\char 12\relax{X}{T}t\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Type abstraction)}\\[2.13394pt]
\text{Ground value}&v_{G}&\Coloneqq&\texttt{()}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Unit value)}\\
&&|&0\;\;|\;\;1\;\;|\;\;2\;\;|\;\;\ldots\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Integer)}\\
&&|&\texttt{true}\;\;|\;\;\texttt{false}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Boolean)}\\
&&|&\text{"{Hello}"}\;\;|\;\;\text{"{World}"}\;\;|\;\;\ldots\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(String)}\\
&&|&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\texttt{b}(42)\;\;|\;\;\texttt{b}(192,168,1,1)\;\;|\;\;\ldots}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(Bytes)}}$}}\\
&&|&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${a_{T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}}}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(Server address)}}$}}\\
&&|&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${s_{T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}}}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(Client-server channel)}}$}}\\[2.13394pt]
&&|&{v_{G}}\mathbin{\texttt{{:}\!{:}}}{v_{G}}\;\;|\;\;\texttt{nil}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(List of ground values)}\\
&&|&\text{$\{f_{i}=v_{Gi}\}_{i\in I}$}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Record of ground values)}\\
\text{Type}&T&\Coloneqq&\top\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Top type)}\\
&&|&\text{Int}\;|\;\text{Bool}\;|\;\text{String}\;|\;\text{Unit}\;|\;\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{\text{Bytes}}}\;|\;\ldots\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Basic types)}\\
&&|&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{ServerRef}[T,T,T]}$}}\;\;|\;\;\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{Chan}[T,T,T]}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(P4Runtime server address \& channel)}}$}}\\
&&|&\text{$\{f_{i}:T_{i}\}_{i\in I}$}\;\;|\;\;[T]\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Record and list types)}\\
&&|&T\rightarrow T\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Function type)}\\
&&|&X\;\;|\;\;\forall X<:T.\ T\;\;|\;\;T\ T\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Type var., bounded quant., application)}\\
&&|&{T}\cup{T}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Union type)}\\
&&|&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\underline{v_{G}}}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(Singleton type for ground value $v_{G}$)}}$}}\\
&&|&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{$T\texttt{ match }\{T_{i}\Rightarrow T^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in I}$}}$}}\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\text{(Match type)}}$}}\\
\end{array}

Figure 3
.
Syntax of
F_{\text{P4R}}
terms and types. Non-standard extensions to
F_{<:}
are
highlighted
.

Most of
Definition

3.1
is based on standard
F_{<:}
constructs
and extensions (in particular, lists and records). The key deviations are the

highlighted
constructs in
Figure

3
:

•

a
P4Runtime operation
\mathit{op}
allows a client to connect
to a P4Runtime server, and query or change the entries in its configuration;

•

a
ground value
v_{G}
is a value that does not contain
lambda nor type abstractions. A ground value is a “simple” value
(e.g. string, integer, …), or a list or record of ground values. For
each ground value
v_{G}
, there is a
singleton type

\underline{v_{G}}
only inhabited by
v_{G}
itself;

•

a
byte string
\texttt{b}(\ldots)
is the byte representation of a
sequence of integers, and has type
Bytes
;

•

a
server address
a_{T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}}
represents a handle for
connecting to a P4Runtime server (in practice, it represents its IP address
and TCP port). A server address
a_{T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}}
has a corresponding

server address type

\text{ServerRef}[T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}]
, where the type
parameters reflect information available in the server’s P4Info
file:
1
1

1

The instantiation of the type parameters
T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}
is detailed later,
in
Example

4.5
,
Definition

5.2
and
Example

5.3
.

–

T_{m}
describes the
matches
of each table in the server
configuration;

–

T_{a}
describes the
actions
that could be performed after
a network packet is matched;

–

T_{p}
describes the types of the
parameters
expected by each
action.

For brevity, we will often just write
a
, omitting the subscript;

•

a
client-server connection
s_{T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}}
represents a
communication channel (created after establishing a connection) that a
P4Runtime server and client use to communicate. A connection value has a
corresponding
channel type

\text{Chan}[T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}]
, whose type
arguments have the same meaning outlined above for

\text{ServerRef}[T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}]
. For brevity, we will often just write
s
,
omitting the subscript;

•

a
match type
describes a type-level pattern matching, as
illustrated in
Example

3.2
below.

Example 3.2 (Match Types).

Consider the following match type:

\text{Int}\;\texttt{match}\;\{\text{Int}\Rightarrow\text{Bool},\;\text{String}\Rightarrow\text{Unit}\}

We call the types
Bool
and
Unit
(i.e., the types of the expressions that can be executed after a match case is selected)
continuation types
of the match type.
This match type “reduces” to the continuation type
Bool
, because its
guard matches the type
Int
. (More precisely, in
Section

4

we will see that the match type and the selected continuation are
subytping-equivalent.)
Now consider the following match type abstracted over type
T
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\text{Table\_Actions}&\triangleq&\forall T.\ T\texttt{ match }\{\\
&&\qquad\underline{\texttt{"IPv4\_table"}}\Rightarrow\underline{\texttt{"IPv4\_forward"}}\cup\underline{\texttt{"drop"}}\\
&&\qquad\underline{\texttt{"IPv6\_table"}}\Rightarrow\underline{\texttt{"IPv6\_forward"}}\cup\underline{\texttt{"drop}}\\
&&\qquad\underline{\texttt{"*"}}\Rightarrow\underline{\text{{"*"}}}\;\;\}\end{array}

Intuitively, the type
\text{Table\_Actions}\ \underline{\texttt{"IPv4\_table"}}

(i.e. type
Table_Actions
applied to the singleton type
\underline{\texttt{"IPv4\_table"}}
)
“reduces” to the continuation type
\underline{\texttt{"IPv4\_forward"}}\cup\underline{\texttt{"drop"}}
(which is a union of singleton types). Instead,
\text{Table\_Actions}\ \underline{\texttt{"*"}}
“reduces” to the singleton type

\underline{\texttt{"*"}}
.

\small\begin{array}[]{rcl}\forall X.T&\triangleq&\forall X<:\top.\ T\\
\text{Option}&\triangleq&\forall A.\ {\{\text{some}:A\}}\cup{\{\text{none}:\text{Unit}\}}\\
\text{TableEntry}&\triangleq&\forall T_{m}.\ \forall T_{a}.\ \forall T_{p}.\ \forall X_{n}.\ \forall X_{a}<:T_{a}\ X_{n}.\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\;}l@{}}\text{name}:X_{n},&\text{matches}:T_{m}\ X_{n},\\
\text{action}:X_{a},&\text{params}:T_{p}\ X_{a}\end{array}\right\}\\
\text{P4Entity}&\triangleq&\forall T_{m}.\ \forall T_{a}.\ \forall T_{p}.\ \forall X_{n}.\ \forall X_{a}<:T_{a}\ X_{n}.\ \left(\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\text{TableEntry}\ T_{m}\ T_{a}\ T_{p}\ X_{n}\ X_{a}\\
\vphantom{x}\cup\ldots\end{array}\right)\end{array}\vskip-8.53581pt

Figure 4
.
Syntactic sugar and abbreviations for
F_{\text{P4R}}
types (based on
Figure

3
).

We complement the syntax of
F_{\text{P4R}}
with the abbreviations in

Figure

4
— which introduces, in particular, these
P4Runtime-specific aliases:

•

“
TableEntry
” is a record type describing a P4 table entry,
as outlined in
Sections

2.2
and
2.3
:

–

the field “name” is a table name, “action” is the action to
perform (using the given “params”) whenever one of the “matches” is
satisfied;

–

the type arguments enforce dependencies between fields, as we will see
in
Section

4
;

•

“
P4Entity
” is the union of different record types,
each one representing a different type of P4 entity that could
be returned when issuing a “read” request to a P4Runtime server.
In this paper we focus on entities of type
TableEntry

(other types of entities are elided).

3.2.
P4Runtime Server Model

We now introduce our abstract model of P4Runtime server
(
Definition

3.3
). The intuition is: a server
S
models the
behaviour of a P4Runtime server with configuration
C
— which, in turn,
represents the information available in the server’s P4Info metadata file, as
outlined in
Sections

2.2
and
2.3
.

Definition 3.3 (P4Runtime Server Model).

A
P4Runtime server
S
is a 4-tuple

\langle C,E,a,K\rangle
where:

•

the
configuration
C
is a mapping that contains the following fields:

–

\mathit{table\_matches}
(abbreviated
\mathit{tm}
),
a mapping from table names to

match fields
, which in turn consist of:

*

\mathit{name}
, the name of the network packet field to inspect;

*

\mathit{type}
, the type of packet matching algorithm used for this packet field
(either
Exact
,

Ternary
,
LPM
,
Range
, or
Optional

(
P4.org Working Group 2020a
)
).

–

\mathit{table\_actions}
(abbreviated
\mathit{ta}
),
mapping table names to sets of allowed action names;

–

\mathit{action\_params}
(abbreviated
\mathit{ap}
),
mapping action names to sets of

action parameters
:

*

\mathit{name}
, the name of the parameter;

*

\mathit{bitwidth}
, the size of the parameter.

•

E
is a set of
P4Runtime entities
e
that can be hosted on
a P4Runtime server. The main type of entity is a
table
entry
,
2
2

2

P4Runtime models several other types of entries, but they
are out of scope for this work.
which is a record consisting of:

–

\mathit{table\_name}
, the name of table which owns the entry;

–

\mathit{field\_matches}
, a set of records describing packet matching rules:

*

\mathit{name}
, the name of the network packet field to inspect;

*

a set of additional key-value entries, depending on the type
of packet matching algorithm used for this field (for example,
when using the matching algorithm type

Range
, the key-value entries are called
{}^{\prime}\texttt{low}^{\prime}
and
{}^{\prime}\texttt{high}^{\prime}
);

–

\mathit{action\_name}
, the name of the action that the entry
applies upon network packet match;

–

\mathit{action\_args}
, a set of
action argument
records,
which in turn contains:

*

\mathit{name}
, the name of the associated parameter;

*

\mathit{value}
, the value provided as the argument;

•

a
is the
address
where the server listens for connections;

•

K
is a set of
channels
: the active connections between the
server and its clients.

As mentioned in the opening of
Section

3
, the
P4Runtime server model formalised in
Definition

3.3
focuses
on the configuration of a P4Runtime server, by abstracting from other
implementation details (e.g. its implementation language). An example
server configuration can be seen in
Figure

5
.

\small\begin{array}[]{rcl}C.\mathit{table\_matches}&=&\{\texttt{"IPv4\_table"}\mapsto\{\mathit{name}\mapsto\texttt{"IPv4\_dst\_addr"},\mathit{type}\mapsto\texttt{LPM}\},\\
&&\;\;\texttt{"IPv6\_table"}\mapsto\{\mathit{name}\mapsto\texttt{"IPv6\_dst\_addr"},\mathit{type}\mapsto\texttt{LPM}\}\}\\
C.\mathit{table\_actions}&=&\{\texttt{"IPv4\_table"}\mapsto[\texttt{"IPv4\_forward"},\texttt{"Drop"}],\\
&&\;\;\texttt{"IPv6\_table"}\mapsto[\texttt{"IPv6\_forward"},\texttt{"Drop"}]\}\\
C.\mathit{action\_params}&=&\{\texttt{"IPv4\_forward"}\mapsto[\;\{\mathit{name}\mapsto\texttt{"mac\_dst"},\mathit{bitwidth}\mapsto 48\},\\
&&\phantom{\;\;\texttt{"IPv4\_forward"}\mapsto[\;}\{\mathit{name}\mapsto\texttt{"port"},\mathit{bitwidth}\mapsto 9\}\;]\\
&&\;\;\texttt{"IPv6\_forward"}\mapsto[\;\{\mathit{name}\mapsto\texttt{"mac\_dst"},\mathit{bitwidth}\mapsto 48\},\\
&&\phantom{\;\;\texttt{"IPv6\_forward"}\mapsto[\;}\{\mathit{name}\mapsto\texttt{"port"},\mathit{bitwidth}\mapsto 9\}\;]\\
&&\;\;\texttt{"Drop"}\mapsto[]\;\}\end{array}

Figure 5
.
Example of a P4Runtime server configuration
C
(by

Definition

3.3
). This JSON-like structure models the
P4Info metadata describing the configuration of an actual P4 device (as
outlined in
Sections

2.2
and
2.3
).

3.3.
P4Runtime Networks

We conclude this section by formalising the syntax of a network of P4Runtime
servers and clients.

Definition 3.4 (P4Runtime Network).

A
P4Runtime network
is a parallel composition of clients (i.e. terms

t
of the grammar in
Definition

3.1
) and servers
(i.e. tuples
S
conforming to
Definition

3.3
):

N\quad\Coloneqq\quad t\;\;\;|\;\;\;S\;\;\;|\;\;\;N\,|\,N

4.
The
F_{\text{P4R}}
Typing System

Definitions

4.1
and
4.2
formalise the typing system of

F_{\text{P4R}}
. The typing system is based on System
F_{<:}

(
Cardelli et al. 1994
)

extended with singleton types and match types

(
Blanvillain et al. 2022
)
, plus new typing rules for the P4Runtime-specific
operations we introduced in
Definition

3.1
.

Definition 4.1 (Typing Environment).

A
typing environment
\Gamma
is a mapping from term or type variables
to types, that we syntactically represent as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\Gamma&\Coloneqq&\emptyset&\text{(Empty typing environment)}\\
&|&\Gamma,\,x:T&\text{(Term variable $x$ has type $T$)}\\
&|&\Gamma,\,X<:T&\text{(Type variable $X$ has upper bound $T$)}\\
\end{array}

Definition 4.2 (The
F_{\text{P4R}}
Typing System).

The
F_{\text{P4R}}
typing system
consists of the following
mutually-defined, inductive judgements:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad}l@{\hspace{-10mm}}l}\vdash\Gamma\ \text{env}&\text{($\Gamma$ is a valid typing environment)}\hfil\hskip-28.45274pt&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:typing-env-valid})}\\
\Gamma\vdash T\ \text{type}&\text{($T$ is a valid type in $\Gamma$)}\hfil\hskip-28.45274pt&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:type-valid})}\\
\Gamma\vdash T\diamond T^{\prime}&\text{(Types $T$ and $T^{\prime}$ are disjoint in $\Gamma$)}\hfil\hskip-28.45274pt&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:type-disjoint})}\\
\Gamma\vdash T<:T^{\prime}&\text{($T$ is subtype of $T^{\prime}$ in $\Gamma$ --- assuming $\Gamma\vdash T\ \text{type}$ and $\Gamma\vdash T^{\prime}\ \text{type}$)}\hfil\hskip-28.45274pt&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:subtyping-rules})}\\
\Gamma\vdash T=:=T^{\prime}&\text{($T$ and $T^{\prime}$ are subtyping-equivalent in $\Gamma$, i.e.\penalty\ $\Gamma\vdash T<:T^{\prime}$ and $\Gamma\vdash T^{\prime}<:T$)}\hfil\hskip-28.45274pt\\
\Gamma\vdash t:T&\text{($t$ has type $T$ in $\Gamma$ --- assuming $\Gamma\vdash T\ \text{type}$)}\hfil\hskip-28.45274pt&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:typing-rules})}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\envJ{\emptyset}}\ \textsc{Env-$\emptyset$}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typeJ{\Gamma}{T} \quad x \not\in\dom{\Gamma}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\envJ{\Gamma, \types{x}{T}}}\ \textsc{Env-V}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typeJ{\Gamma}{T} \quad X \not\in\dom{\Gamma}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\envJ{\Gamma, X <: T}}\ \textsc{Env-TV}\end{array}

Figure 6
.
Typing environment validity rules.

\small\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\envJ{\Gamma}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\singleton{v_G}}}\ \textsc{Type-Val}}$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\envJ{\Gamma}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\tx{Int}}{}}\ \textsc{Type-Int}\hskip 9.24994pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\envJ{\Gamma}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\tx{Bool}}{}}\ \textsc{Type-Bool}\hskip 9.24994pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\envJ{\Gamma}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\tx{Unit}}{}}\ \textsc{Type-Unit}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\envJ{\Gamma}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\tx{String}}{}}\ \textsc{Type-String}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\envJ{\Gamma}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\tx{Bytes}}{}}\ \textsc{Type-Bytes}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\envJ{\Gamma, \subtypes{X}{T}, \Gamma'}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma, \subtypes{X}{T}, \Gamma'}{X}}\ \textsc{Type-TV}\\[11.38109pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_1} \quad\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_2} \quad\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_3}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\ServerRef{T_1,T_2,T_3}}}\ \textsc{Type-SR}}$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_1} \quad\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_2} \quad\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_3}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\Chan{T_1,T_2,T_3}}}\ \textsc{Type-Chan}}$}}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_1} \quad\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_2}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_1 \rightarrow T_2}}\ \textsc{Type-Abs}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typeJ{\Gamma, \subtypes{X}{T_1}}{T_2}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\quant{X}{T_1} T_2}}\ \textsc{Type-TAbs}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_1} \quad\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_2} \\
\eqtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_1}{\quant{X}{T_{11}} T_{12}} \quad\typeJ{\Gamma}{\quant{X}{T_{11}} T_{12}} \quad\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_2}{T_{11}}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_1\ T_2}}\ \textsc{Type-TApp}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\forall i \in I : \typeJ{\Gamma}{T_i}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\curl{f_i : T_i}_{i \in I}}}\ \textsc{Type-Rec}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typeJ{\Gamma}{T}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{[T]}}\ \textsc{Type-List}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_1} \quad\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_2}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\typeUnion{T_1}{T_2}}}\ \textsc{Type-Union}\\[11.38109pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typeJ{\Gamma}{T_s} \quad\forall i \in I : \typeJ{\Gamma}{T_i} \quad\forall i \in I : \typeJ{\Gamma}{T'_i}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typeJ{\Gamma}{\match{T_s}{T_i \Rightarrow T'_i}{i \in I}}}\ \textsc{Type-Match}}$}}\end{array}

Figure 7
.
Type validity rules. Non-standard extensions to
F_{<:}
are
highlighted
.

Most type validity rules in
Figure

7
are standard. The exceptions
are
highlighted
:

•

by rule
Type-Val
, any ground value
v_{G}
(i.e. any value that
does
not
contain lambda or type abstractions, by

Definition

3.1
) has a corresponding singleton type

\underline{v_{G}}
;

•

rules
Type-SR
and
Type-Chan
say that our new server
address and client-server channel types are well-formed if all their type
arguments are well-formed;

•

by rule
Type-Match
, a match type is well-formed if the scrutinee
type (
T_{s}
), the types being matched against (
T_{i}
), and the
continuation types
(T^{\prime}_{i})
are well-formed.

\small\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T}{T}}\ \textsc{ST-Refl}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T}{\top}}\ \textsc{ST-$\top$}\hskip 18.49988pt\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\memberOf{v_G}{T}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{\singleton{v_G}}{T}}\ \textsc{ST-Val}}$}}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\envJ{\Gamma, \subtypes{X}{T}, \Gamma'}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\subtypesTo{\Gamma, \subtypes{X}{T}, \Gamma'}{X}{T}}\ \textsc{ST-Var}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_1}{T_2}\quad\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_2}{T_3}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_1}{T_3}}\ \textsc{ST-Trans}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\eqtypesTo{\Gamma}{\typeUnion{T_1}{T_2}}{\typeUnion{T_2}{T_1}}}\ \textsc{ST-$\cup$Comm}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\eqtypesTo{\Gamma}{\typeUnion{(\typeUnion{T_1}{T_2})}{T_3}}{\typeUnion{T_1}{(\typeUnion{T_2}{T_3})}}}\ \textsc{ST-$\cup$Assoc}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_1}{T_2}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{[T_1]}{[T_2]}}\ \textsc{ST-List}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_1}{T_2}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_1}{\typeUnion{T_2}{T_3}}}\ \textsc{ST-$\cup$R}\hskip 9.24994pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\forall i \in I:\ \subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_i}{T_i'}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{\curl{f_i : T_i}_{i \in I}}{\curl{f_i : T_i'}_{i \in I}}}\ \textsc{ST-Rec}\\[11.38109pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_s}{T_k} \quad\forall i \in I: i < k \Rightarrow\disjoint{\Gamma}{T_s}{T_i}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\eqtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_s\ttt{ match }\curl{T_i \Rightarrow T_i'}_{i \in I}}{T_k'}}\ \textsc{ST-Match1}}$}}\\[11.38109pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_s}{T_s'} \quad\forall i \in I: \subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_i'}{T_i''}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_s\ttt{ match }\curl{T_i \Rightarrow T_i'}_{i \in I}}{T_s'\ttt{ match }\curl{T_i \Rightarrow T_i''}_{i \in I}}}\ \textsc{ST-Match2}}$}}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_1'}{T_1}\quad\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_2}{T_2'}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_1 \rightarrow T_2}{T_1' \rightarrow T_2'}}\ \textsc{ST-Abs}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_1'}{T_1}\quad\subtypesTo{\Gamma, X <: T_1'}{T_2}{T_2'}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{(\forall X <: T_1.\ T_2)}{(\forall X <: T_1'.\ T_2')}}\ \textsc{ST-TAbs}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_2}{T_1}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\eqtypesTo{\Gamma}{(\quant{X}{T_1} T_0)\ T_2}{\subs{T_0}{X}{T_2}}}\ \textsc{ST-App}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{minipage}[172.5pt]\small$\text{where:}\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\emptyset[X\mapsto T]&=&\emptyset\\
(\Gamma,x:T^{\prime})[X\mapsto T]&=&(\Gamma[X\mapsto T]),x:T^{\prime}[X\mapsto T]\\
(\Gamma,X^{\prime}<:T^{\prime})[X\mapsto T]&=&(\Gamma[X\mapsto T]),X^{\prime}<:T^{\prime}[X\mapsto T]\end{array}$
\end{minipage}\end{array}

Figure 8
.
Subtyping rules.

The subtyping rules in
Figure

8
are also standard, with the
following
highlighted
exceptions:

•

by rule
ST-Val
, if a ground value
v_{G}
belongs to type
T

(by the relation “
v_{G}\mathrel{\in_{G}}T
” defined in
Section

A.1.4
),
then the singleton type
\underline{v_{G}}
is subtype of
T
. For example:
since
42\in\text{Int}
, then we have
\Gamma\vdash\underline{42}<:\text{Int}

(assuming
\Gamma\vdash\text{Int}\ \text{type}
);

•

rule
ST-Match1
(adapted from
(
Blanvillain et al. 2022
)
) says that
a match type is subtyping-equivalent to the continuation type
T^{\prime}_{k}
if all
match type cases before
k
(i.e. all
T_{i}
with
i<k
) are

disjoint
form
T_{k}
, according to
Definition

4.3
below;

•

rule
ST-Match2
(also adapted from
(
Blanvillain et al. 2022
)
) says
that match types are covariant in both the type being matched, and the
continuation types.

The type disjointness judgement
\Gamma\vdash T_{1}\diamond T_{2}
(used in rule

ST-Match1
) is formalised in
Definition

4.3
below: the
intuition is that two types
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are disjoint when they have no
common subtypes, hence there exists no value that can have both types
T_{1}
and

T_{2}
.

Definition 4.3 (Disjointness of Types).

Two types
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are disjoint in
\Gamma
, written

\Gamma\vdash T_{1}\diamond T_{2}
, iff:

(1)

\Gamma\vdash T_{1}\ \text{type}
and
\Gamma\vdash T_{2}\ \text{type}
;  and

(2)

\not\exists T_{3}:\;\Gamma\vdash T_{3}\ \text{type}\;\text{ and }\;\Gamma\vdash T_{3}<:T_{1}\;\text{ and }\;\Gamma\vdash T_{3}<:T_{2}

Example 4.4 (Subtyping and Disjointness in Match Types).

Consider the following match type:

\forall X.\ X\texttt{ match }\{\text{Int}\Rightarrow\underline{42},\;\text{Bool}\Rightarrow\underline{\text{"{Hello}"}}\}

The type

(\forall X.\ X\texttt{ match }\{\text{Int}\Rightarrow\underline{42},\text{Bool}\Rightarrow\underline{\text{"{Hello}"}}\})\ \underline{\texttt{true}}
is subtyping-equivalent (i.e. “reduces”) to

\underline{\text{"{Hello}"}}
, by the subtyping rule
ST-Match1

in
Figure

8
. The rule
first checks whether
\underline{\texttt{true}}
is a subtype of
Int
,
which it is not. Since it is also disjoint from
Int

(the two types do not share a common subtype),
the rule then proceeds to the next case. Here,

\underline{\texttt{true}}
is a subtype of
Bool
, and so
the type “reduces” to the case
\underline{\text{"{Hello}"}}
.

\small\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{v_G}{\singleton{v_G}}}\ \textsc{T-Val}}$}}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\forall i \in I: \typesTo{\Gamma}{t_i}{T_i}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{\curl{f_i=t_i}_{i \in I}}{\curl{f_i : T_i}_{i \in I}}}\ \textsc{T-Rec}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_0}{T_0} \quad\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_1}{\listT{T_0}}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{\concat{t_0}{t_1}}{\listT{T_0}}}\ \textsc{T-List}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t}{\listT{T}}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{\listHead t}{\tx{Option}\ T}}\ \textsc{T-Head}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t}{\listT{T}}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{\listTail t}{\tx{Option}\ \listT{T}}}\ \textsc{T-Tail}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\envJ{\Gamma, \types{x}{T}, \Gamma'}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma, \types{x}{T}, \Gamma'}{x}{T}}\ \textsc{T-Var}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_0}{T_0} \quad\typesTo{\Gamma, \types{x}{T_0}}{t_1}{T_1}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{\letExp{x}{t_0} t_1}{T_1}}\ \textsc{T-Let}\hskip 9.24994pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma,\types{x}{T_1}}{t}{T_2}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{\abs{x}{T_1} t}{T_1 \rightarrow T_2}}\ \textsc{T-Abs}\hskip 9.24994pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_1}{T_1 \rightarrow T_2} \quad\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_2}{T_1}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_1\ t_2}{T_2}}\ \textsc{T-App}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma,\subtypes{X}{T_1}}{t}{T_2}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{(\tabs{X}{T_1} t)}{(\quant{X}{T_1} T_2)}}\ \textsc{T-TAbs}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_1}{\quant{X}{T_1} T_0} \quad\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_2}{T_1}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_1\ T_2}{(\quant{X}{T_1} T_0)\ T_2}}\ \textsc{T-TApp}\\[11.38109pt]
\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_r}{\curl{\types{f_i}{T_i}}_{i \in I}} \quad k \in I\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_r.f_k}{T_k}}\ \textsc{T-Field}\hskip 18.49988pt\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t}{T} \quad\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T}{T'}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{t}{T'}}\ \textsc{T-Sub}\\[11.38109pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_s}{T_s} \quad\forall i \in I: \typesTo{\Gamma, \types{x_i}{T_i}}{t_i}{T_i'} \quad\subtypesTo{\Gamma}{T_s}{\cup_{i \in I} T_i}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_s \ttt{ match }\curl{x_i : T_i \Rightarrow t_i}_{i \in I}}{T_s \ttt{ match } \curl{T_i \Rightarrow T_i'}_{i \in I}}}\ \textsc{T-Match}}$}}\\[11.38109pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t}{\ServerRef{T_m,T_a,T_p}}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{\ttt{Connect}(t)}{\Chan{T_m,T_a,T_p}}}\ \textsc{T-OpC}}$}}\\[11.38109pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_c}{\Chan{T_m,T_a,T_p}} \quad\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_e}{\PPPPEntity\ T_m\ T_a\ T_p\ X_n\ X_a}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{\ttt{Read}(t_c,t_e)}{[\PPPPEntity\ T_m\ T_a\ T_p\ X_n\ X_a]}}\ \textsc{T-OpR}}$}}\\[11.38109pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_c}{\Chan{T_m,T_a,T_p}} \quad\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_e}{\PPPPEntity\ T_m\ T_a\ T_p\ X_n\ X_a}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{\ttt{Insert}(t_c,t_e)}{\tx{Bool}}}\ \textsc{T-OpI}}$}}\\[11.38109pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_c}{\Chan{T_m,T_a,T_p}} \quad\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_e}{\PPPPEntity\ T_m\ T_a\ T_p\ X_n\ X_a}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{\ttt{Modify}(t_c,t_e)}{\tx{Bool}}}\ \textsc{T-OpM}}$}}\\[11.38109pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_c}{\Chan{T_m,T_a,T_p}} \quad\typesTo{\Gamma}{t_e}{\PPPPEntity\ T_m\ T_a\ T_p\ X_n\ X_a}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\typesTo{\Gamma}{\ttt{Delete}(t_c,t_e)}{\tx{Bool}}}\ \textsc{T-OpD}}$}}\end{array}

Figure 9
.
Typing rules for
F_{\text{P4R}}
terms. Non-standard extensions to
F_{<:}
are
highlighted
.

Finally,
Figure

9
includes several (
highlighted
)
non-standard typing rules for
F_{\text{P4R}}
terms:

•

by rule
T-Val
, a ground value
v_{G}
is typed by the singleton
type
\underline{v_{G}}
. E.g.
42
has type
\underline{42}
, hence (via the
subsumption rule
T-Sub
and
ST-Val
in

Figure

8
) we also have that
42
has type
Int
;

•

by rule
T-Match
(adapted from
(
Blanvillain et al. 2022
)
), a
pattern matching term is typed with a match type of a similar shape. The
clause “
\Gamma\vdash T_{s}<:\cup_{i\in I}T_{i}
” ensures that
pattern matching is exhaustive;

•

by rule
T-OpC
, a connection operation to a server address
t

of type
\text{ServerRef}[T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}]
has channel type
\text{Chan}[T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}]
—
i.e. the type of the channel returned by the connection maintains
type-level information about the server configuration;

•

by rule
T-OpR
, the query operation
\texttt{Read}(t_{c},t_{e})
is typed
as follows:

(1)

the query argument
t_{e}
has type
P4Entity

(
Figure

4
) applied to type parameters that
match those of the type of
t_{c}
(expected to be a channel).
Intuitively, this means that
t_{e}
can only be
a P4 entity supported by the P4Runtime server connected over
t_{c}
; and

(2)

the read operation returns a list of type
P4Entity
applied to
type arguments that match those of the type of
t_{c}
. Intuitively,
this means that the returned list is expected to only contain entities supported by the P4Runtime server
connected via
t_{c}
;

•

rules
T-OpI
,
T-OpM
, and
T-OpD
have type
constraints similar to
T-OpR
above: their argument
t_{e}
must be a
P4 entity supported by the server connected over channel
t_{c}
. All
these operations return a boolean value (indicating whether the operation had
an effect).

Example 4.5 (Typable and Untypable
F_{\text{P4R}}
Operations).

Consider the following types:
3
3

3

You may notice a similarity between the types used in

Example

4.5
and the P4Runtime server
configuration in
Figure

5
:
indeed, those types capture the constraints of that server configuration.
We will reprise the topic in

Section

5.2
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}T_{m}&=&\forall T.\ T\texttt{ match }\{\\
&&\qquad\underline{\text{"{IPv4\_table}"}}\Rightarrow\{\mathit{name}:\underline{\text{"{IPv4\_dst\_addr}"}},\mathit{value}:\text{Bytes},\mathit{prefixLen}:\text{Bytes}\}\\
&&\qquad\underline{\text{"{IPv6\_table}"}}\Rightarrow\{\mathit{name}:\underline{\text{"{IPv6\_dst\_addr}"}},\mathit{value}:\text{Bytes},\mathit{prefixLen}:\text{Bytes}\}\;\}\\
T_{a}&=&\forall T.\ T\texttt{ match }\{\\
&&\qquad\underline{\text{"{IPv4\_table}"}}\Rightarrow{\underline{\text{"{IPv4\_forward}"}}}\cup{\underline{\text{"{Drop}"}}}\\
&&\qquad\underline{\text{"{IPv6\_table}"}}\Rightarrow{\underline{\text{"{IPv6\_forward}"}}}\cup{\underline{\text{"{Drop}"}}}\;\}\\
T_{p}&=&\forall A.\ A\texttt{ match }\{\\
&&\qquad\underline{\text{"{IPv4\_forward}"}}\Rightarrow\{\mathit{mac\_dst}:\text{Bytes},\mathit{port}:\text{Bytes}\}\\
&&\qquad\underline{\text{"{IPv6\_forward}"}}\Rightarrow\{\mathit{mac\_dst}:\text{Bytes},\mathit{port}:\text{Bytes}\}\\
&&\qquad\underline{\text{"{Drop}"}}\Rightarrow\text{Unit}\;\}\\
\end{array}

Assume that the connection
s
has type
\text{Chan}[T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}]
.
Then, we can type the following P4Runtime operation:

\displaystyle\small\texttt{Insert}(s,\{

\displaystyle\text{"{IPv4\_table}"},\;\;\{\mathit{name}=\text{"{IPv4\_dst\_addr}"},\mathit{value}=\texttt{b}(10,1,0,0),\mathit{prefixLen}=32\},

\displaystyle\text{"{IPv4\_forward}"},\;\;\{\mathit{mac\_dst}=\texttt{b}(8,0,0,0,10,1),\mathit{port}=\texttt{b}(1)\}\;\})

The typing succeeds because,
by rule
T-OpI
in
Figure

9
,
the types
T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}
used to instantiate
the type of
s
(i.e.
\text{Chan}[T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}]
) can correctly type the
record passed as second argument of
Insert
. The type of such a record
is
\text{P4Entity}\ T_{m}\ T_{a}\ T_{p}\ \underline{\text{"{IPv4\_table}"}}\ \underline{\text{"{IPv4\_forward}"}}
(see
Figure

4
),
and its type-checking succeeds because:

(1)

T_{m}\ \underline{\text{"{IPv4\_table}"}}
reduces to a record type

\{\mathit{name}:\underline{\text{"{IPv4\_dst\_addr}"}},\;\ldots\}

that types the record instance

\{\mathit{name}=\text{"{IPv4\_dst\_addr}"},\;\ldots\}

(i.e. the table match fields);

(2)

T_{a}\ \underline{\text{"{IPv4\_table}"}}
reduces to the union type

{\underline{\text{"{IPv4\_forward}"}}\,}\cup{\,\underline{\text{"{Drop}"}}}
,
which types the string
"
IPv4_forward
"

(i.e. the name of the action applied upon a packet match);

(3)

T_{p}\ \underline{\text{"{IPv4\_forward}"}}
reduces to a record type

\{\mathit{mac\_dst}:\text{Bytes},\;\ldots\}

that types the record instance
\{\mathit{mac\_dst}=\ldots\}

(i.e. the action parameters).

Instead, the following P4Runtime operation cannot be typed:

\displaystyle\small\texttt{Insert}(s,\{

\displaystyle\text{"{IPv4\_table}"},\;\;\{\mathit{name}=\text{"{IPv4\_dst\_addr}"},\mathit{value}=\texttt{b}(10,1,0,0),\mathit{prefixLen}=32\},

\displaystyle\text{"{IPv6\_forward}"},\;\;\{\mathit{mac\_dst}=\texttt{b}(8,0,0,0,10,1),\mathit{port}=\texttt{b}(1)\}\})

The typing fails because
\text{$T_{a}$}\ \underline{\text{"{IPv4\_table}"}}

reduces to
{\underline{\text{"{IPv4\_forward}"}}}\cup{\underline{\text{"{Drop}"}}}
,
which cannot type the string
"
IPv6_forward
"
.
Intuitively this means that, according to type
T_{a}
,

"
IPv6_forward
"

is an invalid action for the table
"
IPv4_forward
"
.

Remark 4.6 (Differences with
Blanvillain et al. 2022
).

Our formulation of match types differs from the original presentation by

Blanvillain et al. 2022
in 3 significant aspects: these differences are
non-trivial and interplay with each other in subtle ways, making our
formalisation and proofs quite challenging.

(1)

Blanvillain et al. 2022
use a
nominal
type system which models
class hierarchies, abstracting from class fields and data. Instead, we need
data in order to represent P4Runtime tables in
F_{\text{P4R}}
and in our
results; moreover, our implementation (
Section

7
) does not
make significant use of class hierarchies. Therefore, unlike

Blanvillain et al. 2022
, we adopt standard data types (records, lists…)
with
structural
typing and subtyping, and we support singleton types
— and consequently, we adapt the match-typing-related rules accordingly.

(2)

Unlike
Blanvillain et al. 2022
, our match types do
not
include a
mandatory default case. With the default case, a match type can be
“reduced” (i.e. proven subtype-equivalent) to the type in its default case,
if the scrutinee type does not match any other case. We removed the mandatory
default case because it is not needed (and is actually undesirable) for our
modelling of P4Runtime table types. Moreover, the Scala 3 compiler does

not
require programmers to specify a default case in their match types
— and since our API
P4R-Type
leverages this feature, we formalised the
typing system of
F_{\text{P4R}}
accordingly. A default match type case can be
obtained (when needed) by adding a branch that matches the top type
\top
.

(3)

Correspondingly, our match terms do
not
include a mandatory
default case (unlike
Blanvillain et al. 2022
). Consequently, our typing rule

T-Match
(
Figure

9
) has an additional constraint
w.r.t.
Blanvillain et al. 2022
: the scrutinee type must be a subtype of the
union of all case types, thus ensuring that the pattern matching is exhaustive
(the Scala 3 compiler performs similar checks). Notably, match term
exhaustiveness is needed to prove progress (
Theorem

6.4
); instead,

Blanvillain et al. 2022
do not check match term exhaustiveness because their
default match case ensures that a match term can always be reduced.

5.
Semantics of
F_{\text{P4R}}
Programs and P4Runtime Networks

In this section we formalise the semantics of

F_{\text{P4R}}
programs (
Section

5.1
), P4Runtime servers
(
Section

5.2
), and networks of clients and servers
(
Section

5.3
).

5.1.
Semantics of
F_{\text{P4R}}
Programs

We introduce the labelled transition system (LTS) semantics of

F_{\text{P4R}}
.
Definition

5.1
below formalises an

early
semantics, where each transition label denotes either an internal
computation (
\tau
), or a possible input/output interaction with the
surrounding environment. This style of
early
LTS semantics is inspired
by the
\pi
-calculus
(
Sangiorgi and Walker 2001
)
, and
allows us to formalise and reason about the interactions between
F_{\text{P4R}}
programs and P4Runtime servers (formalised later in

Definition

5.7
) while keeping the respective syntax and
semantics decoupled.

Definition 5.1 (Semantics of
F_{\text{P4R}}
).

Assume a predicate “
v\in T
” which holds iff value
v
belongs to
type
T
. We define the
labelled
transition system (LTS) semantics
of
F_{\text{P4R}}
as a
transition relation
t\xrightarrow{\alpha}t^{\prime}
, where the label
\alpha
is defined as:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;\;}r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{\quad}l}\text{Transition label}&\alpha&\Coloneqq&\tau&\text{(Internal transition)}\\
&&|&{\text{connect}(a)}\leadsto{s}&\text{(Connect to server address $a$, getting channel $s$)}\\
&&|&{\text{read}(s,v)}\leadsto{v^{\prime}}&\text{(Perform query $v$ on channel $s$, getting result $v^{\prime}$)}\\
&&|&{\text{insert}(s,v)}\leadsto{v^{\prime}}&\text{(Insert $v$ on channel $s$, getting result $v^{\prime}$)}\\
&&|&{\text{modify}(s,v)}\leadsto{v^{\prime}}&\text{(Modify $v$ on channel $s$, getting result $v^{\prime}$)}\\
&&|&{\text{delete}(s,v)}\leadsto{v^{\prime}}&\text{(Delete $v$ on channel $s$, getting result $v^{\prime}$)}\\
\end{array}

The transition relation
t\xrightarrow{\alpha}t^{\prime}
is defined in

Figure

10
, where the context transition rule
E-
\mathbb{C}
uses
an
evaluation context
\mathbb{C}
(defined below) which represents a

F_{\text{P4R}}
term with one hole
[ ]
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{C}&\Coloneqq&\texttt{[\,]}\;\;|\;\;{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\texttt{{:}\!{:}}}{t}\;\;|\;\;{v}\mathbin{\texttt{{:}\!{:}}}{\mathbb{C}}\;\;|\;\;\texttt{head }{\mathbb{C}}\;\;|\;\;\texttt{tail }{\mathbb{C}}\;\;|\;\;\texttt{let }x\texttt{ = }\mathbb{C}\texttt{ in }t\\
&|&\mathbb{C}\;t\;\;|\;\;v\;\mathbb{C}\;\;|\;\;\mathbb{C}\;T\;\;|\;\;\mathbb{C}.f\;\;|\;\;\mathbb{C}\texttt{ match }\{x_{i}:T_{i}\Rightarrow t_{i}\}_{i\in I}\\
&|&\{f_{i}=\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in I}\quad\text{where\; }\exists k\in I:\forall i\in I:\begin{cases}i<k\text{ \;implies\; }\gamma_{i}=v_{i}\\
i=k\text{ \;implies\; }\gamma_{i}=\mathbb{C}\\
i>k\text{ \;implies\; }\gamma_{i}=t_{i}\end{cases}\end{array}

\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{(\abs{x}{T} t)\ v \reduce{\tau} \subs{t}{x}{v}}\ \textsc{E-App}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{(\tabs{X}{T_1} t)\ T_2 \reduce{\tau} \subs{t}{X}{T_2}}\ \textsc{E-TApp}

\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\letExp{x}{v} t \reduce{\tau} \subs{t}{x}{v}}\ \textsc{E-Let}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}v = \curl{f_i=v_i}_{i \in I} \quad k \in I\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{v.f_k \reduce{\tau} v_k}\ \textsc{E-Field}

\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\listHead\concat{v_0}{v_1} \reduce{\tau} \curl{\tx{some} = v_0}}\ \textsc{E-Head1}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\listHead\nil\reduce{\tau} \curl{\tx{none} = \unit}}\ \textsc{E-Head2}

\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\listTail\concat{v_0}{v_1} \reduce{\tau} \curl{\tx{some} = v_1}}\ \textsc{E-Tail1}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\listTail\nil\reduce{\tau} \curl{\tx{none} = \unit}}\ \textsc{E-Tail2}

\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}k \in I \quad v \in T_k \quad\forall j \in I: j < k \implies v \not\in T_j
\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{v \ttt{ match }\curl{\types{x_i}{T_i} \Rightarrow t_i}_{i \in I} \;\reduce{\tau}\; \subs{t_k}{x_k}{v}}\ \textsc{E-Match}

{\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}a \in\ServerRef{T_m, T_a, T_p} \quad s \in\Chan{T_m, T_a, T_p}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\ttt{Connect}(a) \reduceTwo{\tx{connect}(a)}{s} s}\ \textsc{E-Connect}}

{\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}s \in\Chan{T_m,T_a,T_p} \quad v' \in\listT{\PPPPEntity\ T_m\ T_a\ T_p\ X_n\ X_a}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\ttt{Read}(s,v) \reduceTwo{\tx{read}(s,v)}{v'} {v'}}\ \textsc{E-Read}}

{\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}v' \in\tx{Bool}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\ttt{Insert}(s,v) \reduceTwo{\tx{insert}(s,v)}{v'} {v'}}\ \textsc{E-Insert}}
{\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}v' \in\tx{Bool}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\ttt{Modify}(s,v) \reduceTwo{\tx{modify}(s,v)}{v'} {v'}}\ \textsc{E-Modify}}

\displaystyle\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}{${\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}v' \in\tx{Bool}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\ttt{Delete}(s,v) \reduceTwo{\tx{delete}(s,v)}{v'} {v'}}\ \textsc{E-Delete}}$}}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}t \reduce{\alpha} t'\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\mbC[t] \reduce{\alpha} \mbC[t']}\ \textsc{E-$\mathbb{C}$}

Figure 10
.
LTS semantics of
F_{\text{P4R}}
terms. Non-standard extensions to
F_{<:}
are
highlighted
.

Most rules in
Definition

5.1
are standard, except for the
ones
highlighted
in
Figure

10
:

•

by rule
E-Connect
, the term
\texttt{Connect}(a)
transitions by
producing a channel
s
, whose type conforms to the type of the server
address
a
. The transition label “
{\text{connect}(a)}\leadsto{s}
”
means that the term is trying to interact with the surrounding environment:
hence, as we will see in
Section

5.2
, the
client expects a P4Runtime server to emit the dual label
“
\overline{{\text{connect}(a)}\leadsto{s}}
” — meaning that the server
is listening on address
a
and can produce channel
s
;

•

by rule
E-Read
, the term
\texttt{Read}(s,v)
transitions by
producing a value
v^{\prime}
, which is a list of
P4Entity
instances
(
Figure

4
) whose type conforms to the type of
channel
s
. The transition label means that the term expects to interact
with a P4Runtime server on channel
s
;

•

rules
E-Insert
,
E-Modify
, and
E-Delete
work
similarly, and produce a boolean value describing whether the operation
had an effect or not.

F_{\text{P4R}}
terms are evaluated from left to right, using the evaluation
contexts
\mathbb{C}
in
Definition

5.1
. For instance, the
last case “
\{f_{i}=\gamma_{i}\}_{i\in I}
” represents a record whose fields

f_{i}
are indexed by
i\in I
, where
I
is a set of consecutive natural
numbers
1..n
(as per
Definition

3.1
); all fields to the
left of
f_{k}
(for some
k\in I
) are already fully-evaluated into values

v_{i}
; the field
f_{k}
is a context with a hole, which is going to be evaluated
next; and all fields to the right of
f_{k}
are arbitrary terms
t_{i}
, which may
be evaluated after
f_{k}
.

5.2.
Semantics of P4Runtime Servers

To define our P4Runtime server semantics (in

Definition

5.6
later on), we need to ensure that a server

S
will only answer to well-typed requests from its clients, and that the
server entities are well-typed w.r.t. the server configuration
C
. To this
end, we formalise an encoding of a server configuration
C
into
F_{\text{P4R}}
types (
Definition

5.2
below). Intuitively, this describes
how to turn the P4Info metadata of a P4 device into a set of types describing
the device tables, actions, etc.

Definition 5.2 (Encoding of a Server Configuration into
F_{\text{P4R}}
Types).

Given a P4Runtime server configuration
C
, we define the
encoding

\llbracket\cdots\rrbracket
of its entries into
F_{\text{P4R}}
types in
Figure

11
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}rcl@{}}\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_matches}\rrbracket&=&\forall T.\ T\texttt{ match }\{\\
&&\qquad\underline{t}\Rightarrow\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_matches}(t)\rrbracket,\\
&&\qquad\underline{\texttt{"*"}}\Rightarrow\underline{\texttt{"*"}}\\
&&\}_{\mathit{t}\in dom(C.\mathit{table\_matches})}\\
\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_matches}(t)\rrbracket&=&{\{\mathit{mf}.name:\llbracket\mathit{mf}.type\rrbracket\}_{\mathit{mf}\in C.\mathit{table\_matches}(t)}}\cup{\underline{\texttt{"*"}}}\\
\llbracket\texttt{Exact}\rrbracket&=&\{\mathit{value}:\text{Bytes}\}\\
\llbracket\texttt{Ternary}\rrbracket&=&\text{Option}\ \{\mathit{value}:\text{Bytes},\mathit{mask}:\text{Bytes}\}\\
\llbracket\texttt{LPM}\rrbracket&=&\text{Option}\ \{\mathit{value}:\text{Bytes},\mathit{prefixLen}:\text{Int}\}\\
\llbracket\texttt{Range}\rrbracket&=&\text{Option}\ \{\mathit{low}:\text{Bytes},\mathit{high}:\text{Bytes}\}\\
\llbracket\texttt{Optional}\rrbracket&=&\text{Option}\ \{\mathit{value}:\text{Bytes}\}\\
\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_actions}\rrbracket&=&\forall T.\ T\texttt{ match }\{\\
&&\qquad\underline{t}\Rightarrow\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_actions}(t)\rrbracket,\\
&&\qquad\underline{\texttt{"*"}}\Rightarrow\underline{\texttt{"*"}}\\
&&\}_{\mathit{t}\in dom(C.\mathit{table\_actions})}\\
\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_actions}(t)\rrbracket&=&\bigcup_{a\in C.\mathit{table\_actions}(t)}\underline{a}\\
\llbracket C.\mathit{action\_params}\rrbracket&=&\forall A.\ A\texttt{ match }\{\\
&&\qquad\underline{a}\Rightarrow\llbracket C.\mathit{action\_params}(a)\rrbracket,\\
&&\qquad\underline{\texttt{"*"}}\Rightarrow\text{Unit}\\
&&\}_{\mathit{a}\in dom(C.\mathit{action\_params})}\\
\llbracket C.\mathit{action\_params}(a)\rrbracket&=&\begin{cases}\text{Unit}&\text{if }C.\mathit{action\_params}(a)=\emptyset\\
\{p.\mathit{name}:\text{Bytes}\}_{p\in C.\mathit{action\_params}(a)}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{array}

Figure 11
.
Definition of the encoding operation
\llbracket\cdots\rrbracket
from P4Runtime configurations to
F_{\text{P4R}}
types.

Example 5.3 (Server Configuration Representation as
F_{\text{P4R}}
Types).

Consider the P4Runtime server configuration in

Figure

5
: by
Definition

5.2
,
its encoding into the
F_{\text{P4R}}
types is shown in

Figure

12
.
(The same types are also used in

Example

4.5
, where they are called

T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}
.)

\small\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_matches}\rrbracket&=&\forall T.\ T\texttt{ match }\{\\
&&\hskip 18.49988pt\underline{\texttt{"IPv4\_table"}}\Rightarrow\{\mathit{name}:\underline{\texttt{"IPv4\_dst\_addr"}},\mathit{value}:\text{Bytes},\mathit{prefixLen}:\text{Bytes}\}\\
&&\hskip 18.49988pt\underline{\texttt{"IPv6\_table"}}\Rightarrow\{\mathit{name}:\underline{\texttt{"IPv6\_dst\_addr"}},\mathit{value}:\text{Bytes},\mathit{prefixLen}:\text{Bytes}\}\;\}\\
\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_actions}\rrbracket&=&\forall T.\ T\texttt{ match }\{\\
&&\hskip 18.49988pt\underline{\texttt{"IPv4\_table"}}\Rightarrow{\underline{\texttt{"IPv4\_forward"}}}\cup{\underline{\texttt{"Drop"}}}\\
&&\hskip 18.49988pt\underline{\texttt{"IPv6\_table"}}\Rightarrow{\underline{\texttt{"IPv6\_forward"}}}\cup{\underline{\texttt{"Drop"}}}\;\}\\
\llbracket C.\mathit{action\_params}\rrbracket&=&\forall A.\ A\texttt{ match }\{\\
&&\hskip 18.49988pt\underline{\texttt{"IPv4\_forward"}}\Rightarrow\{\mathit{mac\_dst}:\text{Bytes},\mathit{port}:\text{Bytes}\}\\
&&\hskip 18.49988pt\underline{\texttt{"IPv6\_forward"}}\Rightarrow\{\mathit{mac\_dst}:\text{Bytes},\mathit{port}:\text{Bytes}\}\\
&&\hskip 18.49988pt\underline{\texttt{"Drop"}}\Rightarrow\text{Unit}\;\}\\
\end{array}

Figure 12
.
The encoding of the server configuration
C
in

Figure

5
into
F_{\text{P4R}}
types.
(The same types are also used in

Example

4.5
, where they are called

T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}
.)

From now on, we will assume that each P4Runtime server is
well-formed
by

Definition

5.4
below: it means that each entity belongs
to the
P4Entity
type (
Figure

4
) instantiated
with type parameters that correspond to the type-encoded server configuration
(by
Definition

5.2
).

Definition 5.4 (P4Runtime Entity Conformity and Server Well-Formedness).

A P4Runtime entity
e

conforms
to a server configuration
C
iff:

\exists X_{n},X_{a}:e\in\text{P4Entity}\ \llbracket C.\mathit{table\_matches}\rrbracket\ \llbracket C.\mathit{table\_actions}\rrbracket\ \llbracket C.\mathit{action\_params}\rrbracket\ X_{n}\ X_{a}

The predicate
\mathit{Conforms}(e,C)
holds iff entity
e
conforms to the
configuration
C
.
A P4Runtime server
\langle C,E,a,K\rangle
is well-formed

iff
\forall e\in E:\mathit{Conforms}(e,C)
.

The key insight behind
Definition

5.4
is that the
instantiation of
P4Entity
can only reduce to an actual type if the argument

X_{n}
is a valid table name in
C
, and if
X_{a}
is a valid action for table

X_{n}
.

Definition

5.4
directly leads to the following property,
which will allow us to prove the results in
Section

6
: if a client
sends to the server a well-typed value
v
, the server will consider it conformant.

Proposition 5.5 (Conformance of Well-Typed Values).

For any server
S=\langle C,E,a,K\rangle
and any value
v
, we have:

\mathit{Conforms}(v,C)\iff\emptyset\vdash v:\text{P4Entity}\ \llbracket C.\mathit{table\_matches}\rrbracket\ \llbracket C.\mathit{table\_actions}\rrbracket\ \llbracket C.\mathit{action\_params}\rrbracket\ X_{n}\ X_{a}

Definition 5.6 (P4Runtime Server Semantics).

We define the
semantics of a P4Runtime server
S
as a relation
S\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}}S^{\prime}
(where
\alpha
is from

Definition

5.1
) inductively defined by the rules in

Figure

13
.

\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}s \tx{ is a fresh channel} \\[-1mm]
s \in\tx{Chan}[\encoded{C.\mathit{table\_matches}}, \encoded{C.\mathit{table\_actions}}, \encoded{C.\mathit{action\_params}}]\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\serverRed{C, E, a, K}{\tx{connect}(a)}{s}{C, E, a, K \cup\curl{s}}}\ \textsc{Sv-Connect}

\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}s \in K \quad\mathit{Conforms}(v,C) \quad\serverRedInt{C}{E}{\tx{read}(v)}{v'}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\serverRed{C, E, a, K}{\tx{read}(s,v)}{v'}{C, E, a, K}}\ \textsc{Sv-Read}

\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}s \in K \quad\mathit{Conforms}(v,C) \quad\serverRedInt{C}{E}{\tx{insert}(v)}{(E',v')}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\serverRed{C, E, a, K}{\tx{insert}(s,v)}{v'}{C, E', a, K}}\ \textsc{Sv-Insert}

\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}s \in K \quad\mathit{Conforms}(v,C) \quad\serverRedInt{C}{E}{\tx{modify}(v)}{(E',v')}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\serverRed{C, E, a, K}{\tx{modify}(s,v)}{v'}{C, E', a, K}}\ \textsc{Sv-Modify}

\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}s \in K \quad\mathit{Conforms}(v,C) \quad\serverRedInt{C}{E}{\tx{delete}(v)}{(E',v')}\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{\serverRed{C, E, a, K}{\tx{delete}(s,v)}{v'}{C, E', a, K}}\ \textsc{Sv-Delete}

Figure 13
.
LTS semantics of a P4Runtime server.

The P4Runtime server semantics in
Definition

5.6
show how
the internal configuration of a P4Runtime server evolves, and how the server
responds to queries from clients. The semantics are based on the

P4.org Working Group 2020b
.

The server semantics focus on checking the conformance of requests from the
clients, and computes a response using an abstract evaluation predicate
“
\langle\_,\_,\_\rangle\downarrow\_
”: the details of this predicate are not
crucial — but we assume that it always yields a well-typed response, i.e. a
boolean or an entity that conforms to the server configuration
C
.
4
4

4

For reference, the semantics of the predicate “
\langle C,E,\text{read}(v)\rangle\downarrow v^{\prime}
”
are available in the appendix, in
Figure

17
.

•

By rule
Sv-Connect
, a server listening on address
a
can
accept a client connection by generating a unique channel instance
s
,
adding
s
to the set of established connections
K
, and producing a
transition label
\overline{{{\text{connect}(a)}}\leadsto{s}}
.
Importantly, the channels
s
belongs to a
F_{\text{P4R}}
channel type whose
type arguments are obtained by encoding the server configuration
C

(by the encoding
\llbracket\cdots\rrbracket
in
Definition

5.2
).

•

By rule
Sv-Read
, the server can handle a client’s read request
by emitting a label
\overline{{{\text{read}(s,v)}}\leadsto{v^{\prime}}}
, provided
that the connection
s
belongs to the set of established connections
K
,
and the query argument
v
conforms to the server configuration (by

Definition

5.4
);

•

Rules
Sv-Insert
,
Sv-Modify
, and
Sv-Delete

have requirements similar to
Sv-Read
, except that they can modify
the server entities in
E
— e.g. by adding or removing P4 table
entries.

5.3.
Semantics of P4Runtime Networks

We now formalise the semantics of the P4Runtime networks introduced in

Definition

3.4
.

Definition 5.7 (P4Runtime Network Semantics).

The
LTS semantics of a P4Runtime network

is defined by the following rules, where
\alpha
ranges over the labels
introduced in
Definition

5.1
: (for brevity, we omit
the symmetric rules)

\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}N \reduce{\alpha} N'\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{N \,|\, N'' \;\reduce{\alpha}\; N' \,|\, N''}\ \textsc{Net-$\alpha$}\qquad\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\begin{gathered}N_1 \reduce{\dual{\alpha}} N_1' \quad N_2 \reduce{\alpha} N_2'\end{gathered}\end{gathered}}{N_1 \,|\, N_2 \;\reduce{\tau}\; N_1' \,|\, N_2'}\ \textsc{Net-Comm}

We often write
N\xrightarrow{}N^{\prime}
instead of
N\xrightarrow{\tau}N^{\prime}
, and

\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\!*}
for the reflexive and transitive closure of
\xrightarrow{}
.

By
Definition

3.4
, a network
N
is a parallel composition
of any number of P4Runtime clients and servers. According to the semantics in

Definition

5.7
, a network
N
can perform a transition

\alpha
even when composed with another network
N^{\prime\prime}
(rule

Net-
\alpha
); and if two networks fire dual labels
\alpha
and

\overline{\alpha}
, then they can synchronise when composed, producing a

\tau
-transition: this allows a P4Runtime client and server to interact, as
illustrated in
Example

5.8
below.

Example 5.8 (A Simple P4Runtime Network).

We give a brief example of how a network reduction could look using our semantics.
Consider the
F_{\text{P4R}}
term:

\texttt{let }c\texttt{ = }\texttt{Connect}(a)\texttt{ in }\texttt{Insert}(c,v)

This term attempts to connect to a P4Runtime server
a
and insert a value
v

(a P4 table entry). If we compose this
F_{\text{P4R}}
term with a P4Runtime
server, the resulting network reduces as:

\small\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\derive{s \tx{ is a fresh channel} \\[-1mm]
s \in\Chan{\encoded{C.\mathit{tm}}, \encoded{C.\mathit{ta}}, \encoded{C.\mathit{ap}}}}
{\langle C,E,a,K \rangle\;\reduceTwoS{\tx{connect}(a)}{s}\; \langle C,E,a,K \cup\curl{s} \rangle}
\quad\derive{a \in\ServerRef{T_m,T_a,T_p} \quad s \in\Chan{T_m,T_a,T_p}}
{\ttt{Connect}(a) \;\reduceTwo{\tx{connect}(a)}{s}\; s}\end{gathered}}{\langle C,E,a,K \rangle\,|\, \letExp{c}{\ttt{Connect}(a)}\ttt{Insert}(c,v) \;\reduce{\tau}\; \langle C,E,a,K \cup\curl{s} \rangle\,|\, \letExp{c}{s}\ttt{Insert}(c,v) }

Then, the “let” expression substitutes the variable
c
with the channel

s
:

\small\langle C,E,a,K\cup\{s\}\rangle\,|\,\texttt{let }c\texttt{ = }s\texttt{ in }\texttt{Insert}(c,v)\;\xrightarrow{\tau}\;\langle C,E,a,K\cup\{s\}\rangle\,|\,\texttt{Insert}(s,v)

Finally,

the network may reduce as follows: (here, let
K^{\prime}=K\cup\{s\}
and

E^{\prime}=E\cup\{v\}
)

\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{\begin{gathered}\derive{s \in K' \quad\mathit{Conforms}(v, C) \quad\serverRedInt{C}{E}{\tx{insert}(v)}{\ttt{true}}}
{\langle C,E,a,K' \rangle\;\reduceTwoS{\tx{insert}(s,v)}{\ttt{true}}\; \langle C,E',a,K' \rangle}
\quad\derive{\ttt{true} \in\tx{Bool}}
{\ttt{Insert}(s,v) \;\reduceTwo{\tx{insert}(s,v)}{\ttt{true}}\; \ttt{true}}\end{gathered}}{\langle C,E,a,K' \rangle\,|\, \ttt{Insert}(s,v) \;\reduce{\tau}\; \langle C,E',a,K' \rangle\,|\, \ttt{true}}

The above example succeeds because the inserted value
v
conforms to the
server configuration
C
— but this may not always happen: in particular, if

\mathit{Conforms}(v,C)
does
not
hold (e.g. because
v

refers to a P4 table name that does not exist in
C
), then the server and
client cannot synchronise and they get stuck.
To prevent this situation, we introduced the type system for
F_{\text{P4R}}
in
Section

4
, and we prove its properties in
Section

6
.

6.
Results

We now present our main results: well-typed
F_{\text{P4R}}
terms enjoy
type preservation (
Theorem

6.1
) and progress
(
Theorem

6.4
). Type preservation ensures that if a
well-typed
F_{\text{P4R}}
term
t
performs any number of

\tau
-transitions (either with internal computations, or by
interacting with P4Runtime servers in a surrounding network) and
becomes
t^{\prime}
, then
t^{\prime}
is also well-typed with the same type.

Theorem 6.1 (Type preservation).

If
{\Gamma\vdash t:T}
and
t\mid N\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\!*}t^{\prime}\mid N^{\prime}
, then
{\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:T}
.

Our progress result (
Theorem

6.4
) ensures that if a well-typed

F_{\text{P4R}}
term
t
is not a value, then it can perform
further
\tau
-transitions — either through internal computation, or by
interacting with a P4Runtime server in a surrounding network. This second
situation means that a well-typed client and server never get stuck, and
therefore:

•

a well-typed client will never attempt to read or update P4 entities
unsupported by the server, and

•

a well-typed client will support all values returned by a server after
any operation.

For this result to hold, we must ensure that all clients attempting to connect
to a server will succeed, and that any client-server channel has a client-side
type that matches the server configuration. To guarantee this, we consider

well-typed networks
according to
Definition

6.3
below. But
first, we introduce a notion of
network congruence

(
Definition

6.2
below) allowing us to abstract from the order in
which clients and servers appear in a parallel composition.

Definition 6.2 (Network Congruence).

\equiv
is the least congruence between networks such that:

N_{1}\mid N_{2}\,\equiv\,N_{2}\mid N_{1}\qquad(N_{1}\mid N_{2})\mid N_{3}\,\equiv\,N_{1}\mid(N_{2}\mid N_{3})

Definition 6.3 (Well-typed Network).

We say that
a network
N
is well-typed
iff for all P4Runtime clients

t
such that
N\equiv t\mid N_{0}
(for some
N_{0}
), we have:

(1)

\emptyset\vdash t:T
(for some
T
);

(2)

for all server addresses
a
occurring in
t
:

•

there is exactly one server
S=\langle C,E,a,K\rangle

such that
N_{0}\equiv S\mid N_{1}
; and

•

a\in\text{ServerRef}\!\left[\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_matches}\rrbracket,\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_actions}\rrbracket,\llbracket C.\mathit{action\_params}\rrbracket\right]

(3)

for all client-server channels
s
occurring in
t
:

•

there is exactly one server
S=\langle C,E,a,K\rangle

with
s\in K
, and such that
N_{0}\equiv S\mid N_{1}
; and

•

s\in\text{Chan}\!\left[\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_matches}\rrbracket,\llbracket C.\mathit{table\_actions}\rrbracket,\llbracket C.\mathit{action\_params}\rrbracket\right]

We now have all ingredients to formalise progress (
Theorem

6.4
), and
the resulting
Corollary

6.5
: well-typed networks only stop
reducing when all P4Runtime clients terminate successfully.

Theorem 6.4 (Progress).

Take any well-typed network
N
, and take any P4Runtime client
t

such that
N\equiv t\mid N_{0}
(for some
N_{0}
). Then either:

•

t
is fully-reduced into a value; or

•

t\xrightarrow{}t^{\prime}
, and correspondingly,

N\xrightarrow{}N^{\prime}\equiv t^{\prime}\mid N_{0}
with
N^{\prime}
well-typed; or

•

there is a server
S
such that
N_{0}\equiv S\mid N_{1}
and
t\mid S\xrightarrow{}t^{\prime}\mid S^{\prime}
,
and correspondingly,
N\xrightarrow{}N^{\prime}\equiv t^{\prime}\mid S^{\prime}\mid N_{1}
with

N^{\prime}
well-typed.

Corollary 6.5 (Type soundness).

Take any well-typed network
N
. If
N\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\!*}N^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
cannot
perform further
\tau
-transitions, then all P4Runtime clients in
N^{\prime}
are
fully-reduced into values.

7.
Implementation of
P4R-Type
: a Scala 3 API based on
F_{\text{P4R}}

We now outline the implementation
P4R-Type
, our verified API for programming
P4Runtime client applications, based on our formalisation
of
F_{\text{P4R}}
and its typing system (
Sections

3
and
4
).

P4R-Type
is published as companion artifact of this paper, and its latest version
is available at:

https://github.com/JensKanstrupLarsen/P4R-Type/

Our typing system (
Section

4
) is designed to take advantage of
Scala 3 features (in particular, match types
(
Blanvillain et al. 2022
)
): this
naturally leads to implementing
P4R-Type
as a Scala 3 API. Consequently, the
interactions between a client using
P4R-Type
and one or more P4 devices have the
properties presented in
Section

6
: all read/insert/modify/delete
operations are type-safe, and they enjoy progress and preservation (if
both client and device use the same P4Info file).

The implementation of
P4R-Type
consists of:

(1)
a type-parametric API for P4Runtime operations (
connect
,

read
,
insert
, etc.) (
Section

7.1
), and

(2)
a software tool that turns a P4Info file into a set of Scala 3
types
which constrain the
P4R-Type
API
(
Section

7.2
).

7.1.
Type-Parametric API for P4Runtime Operations

The
P4R-Type
API consists of the five P4Runtime operations detailed in

Section

3
:
connect
,
read
,
insert
,
modify
, and

delete
. We implement these operations as methods equipped with the
strict type parameters shown in
Figure

9
(rules
T-OpC
,

T-OpI
,
T-OpM
,
T-OpD
). The operations closely
correspond to the operations in the P4Runtime protobuf API
(
P4.org Working Group 2020b
)
.
Under the hood, these methods use the loosely-typed P4Runtime protobuf
specification and RPC,
5
5

5

https://github.com/p4lang/p4runtime

with (de-)serialisation from/to Scala objects based on the ScalaPB
library:
6
6

6

https://scalapb.github.io/

•

connect
uses the
StreamChannel
RPC to establish a
connection;

•

read
uses the
Read
RPC to read table entries from the
server;

•

insert
,
modify
, and
delete
use the
Write
RPC
to update the server.

The signature of the API methods also align with the formal API:

let
read
=

\displaystyle\lambda T_{m}.\;\lambda T_{a}.\;\lambda T_{p}.\;\char 12\relax{X_{n}}{\text{TableName}}\char 12\relax{X_{a}}{T_{a}\ X_{n}}

\displaystyle\lambda c:\text{Chan}[T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}].\

\displaystyle\lambda x:\{\text{name}:X_{n},\;\text{matches}:T_{m}\ X_{n},\;\text{action}:X_{a},\;\text{params}:T_{p}\ X_{a}\}.\ \texttt{Read}(c,x)

\displaystyle\qquad\texttt{ in }\ldots

⬇

1

def

read
[
TM
[
_
],

TA
[
_
],

TP
[
_
]]

2

(
c
:

FP4Channel
[

,

TA
,

TP
],

tableEntry
:

FP4TableEntry
[

,

,

,

,

])

3

Seq
[

[

,

,

,

,

...

In the code snippet above, the two types
FP4Channel
and

FP4TableEntry
are also part of
P4R-Type
.
Each of these types take the same type parameters as
their equivalents in
Figures

3
and
4
; such type
parameters are usually constrained by the context and inferred by the Scala 3 compiler,
hence the user does not need to write them explicitly. The
FP4Channel
type is simply
a case class that contains the table entry values (table name, parameters,
etc.), while the
FP4Channel
is an abstract class containing the methods
for serialization (
toProto
) and deserialization (
fromProto
).

7.2.
Translation of P4 Device Configuration Metadata (P4Info) into Scala 3 Types

P4R-Type
includes a tool that implements the encoding in

Definition

5.2
: the tool takes a P4Info file
(representing a P4 device’s tables, actions, …) and generates three Scala 3
types, which can be used to instantiate the type parameters
T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}
(see

Sections

3
and
4
) to guarantee type safety and progress. Such
generated types are called
TableMatchFields
,
TableActions
, and

ActionParams
:

•

type
TableMatchFields
can instantiate
T_{m}
, and maps table
names to their match fields;

•

type
TableActions
can instantiate
T_{a}
, and maps table names
to their action names;

•

type
ActionParams
can instantiate
T_{p}
, and maps action names
to their parameter types.

A programmer can use
P4R-Type
to connect to a P4 device and obtain a typed
channel constrained by the 3 types above (akin to
\text{Chan}[T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}]
in

Section

3
); when using our type-parametric API
(
Section

7.1
) on this typed channel, only operations
compatible with the P4 device can be performed; otherwise, a type error occurs
(just like our type system in
Section

4
prevents invalid
operations).

We now illustrate in more detail the
P4R-Type
-generated types that can
instantiate the type parameters
T_{m},T_{a},T_{p}
, using

Figure

12
as an example.

The type parameter
T_{m}
(match fields of a P4 table)

can be instantiated with the higher-kinded type
TableMatchFields
, which
takes a parameter
TN
(expected to be a known table name).

⬇

1

type

TableMatchFields
[
TN
]

=

TN

match

2

case

"IPv4_table"

=>

(
"IPv4_dst_addr"
,

P4
.
LPM
)

3

case

"IPv6_table"

=>

(
"IPv6_dst_addr"
,

P4
.
LPM
)

4

case

"*"

=>

"*"

The type above matches a table name
TN
with one of
the known table names (represented as singleton string types) and yields tuple
types pairing
TN
’s field names with their type of packet match (
P4.Exact
,
P4.Ternary
,

P4.LPM
, …which are types provided by
P4R-Type
). As per P4Runtime
standard, table fields can be optionally undefined, unless they perform a

P4.Exact
packet match.

The type parameter
T_{a}
(P4 table actions)
can be instantiated with type

TableAction
, that matches a table name
TN

to yield the valid actions for
TN
(which may include the wildcard
*
).

⬇

1

type

[
TN
]

<:

ActionName

=

TN

match

2

"IPv4_table"

=>

"IPv4_forward"

"Drop"

"*"

3

case

"IPv6_table"

=>

"IPv6_forward"

|

"Drop"

|

"*"

4

case

"*"

=>

"*"

The type parameter
T_{p}
(action parameters)
can be instantiated with
type
ActionParams
, that matches an action name
AN

to yield the parameter types for
AN
. Each parameter type is a tuple with
the name of the parameter (as a singleton string type) and the value type.

⬇

1

type

[
AN
]

=

AN

match

2

"IPv4_forward"

=>

((
"mac_dst"
,

ByteString
),

(
"port"
,

ByteString
))

3

"IPv6_forward"

=>

((
"mac_dst"
,

ByteString
),

(
"port"
,

ByteString
))

4

case

"Drop"

=>

Unit

5

case

"*"

=>

Unit

All three types above also accept a
wildcard
singleton type
"*"
as
a parameter, representing the request of querying all/any table match fields,
actions, or parameters.

8.
Case Studies and Discussion of Alternative Designs

In this section we demonstrate the usefulness of having compile-time checked
P4Runtime queries, by illustrating three case studies implemented using
P4R-Type
.
We discuss one case study in detail (update of multiple switches, in

Section

8.1
) and outline two more
(port forwarding and load balancing, in
Sections

8.2
and
8.3
):
these applications derive and extend the tunnelling example in
the P4Runtime tutorials,
7
7

7

https://github.com/p4lang/tutorials/tree/master/exercises/p4runtime
and are all included in the software artifact that accompanies this paper.

Figure 15
.
The packet-processing sections of the P4 files of switches S1 and S3 (left) and S2 and S4 (right).

Each switch is configured with a general firewall table for all network
traffic, as well as a more specific IPv4 forwarding table for its own subnet.
For this reason, the switches use different configuration files, shown in

Figure

15
. All of the switches should share the same
entries for the firewall table. Switch S1 is the master switch for forwarding
rules related to subnet
10.1.0.0
, while switch S3 is the master switch
for forwarding rules related to subnet
10.2.0.0
, meaning that S2 and S4
should replicate their table entries, respectively.

The replication of table entries must be done periodically by an external
controller. For this case study, we implement a controller in
P4R-Type
that performs this replication, which should:

(1)

Insert a set of
firewall
table entries into all four switches.

(2)

Read all entries from the
ipv4_lpm
table on S1,
then insert them into S2.

(3)

Read all entries from the
ipv4_table
table on S3,
then insert them into S4.
